################################################################################
PR #904 [OPEN] Test that the energy Hessian is symmetric and DGeod is never positive
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-09-27 11:41 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:energy-hessian-test -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/904
files (3): vmecpp/test_data/BUILD.bazel +16 -0, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/BUILD.bazel +19 -0, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/energy_hessian_test.cc (added) +231 -0

--- description ---
**Change** - adds the Bazel test `//vmecpp/vmec/vmec:energy_hessian_test` and the `circular_tokamak` and `li383_low_res` test-data targets: the derivative of the unpreconditioned force along a displacement, by central difference, is a symmetric form, `<u, H v> = <v, H u>`, for `solovev.json` and `circular_tokamak.json` at `tcon0 = 0`; and `DGeod` is not positive on any interior surface of seven test cases, three-dimensional and asymmetric ones included.

**Cause** - where the force is the gradient of the energy, its derivative is the energy's Hessian, which is symmetric. `DGeod = <G B^2 / |grad s|^3>^2 - <G^2 B^2 / |grad s|^3> <B^2 / |grad s|^3>` is a Cauchy-Schwarz defect, so it is at most zero on every surface.

**Evidence** - symmetric to 1e-6 of the scale for three pairs of displacements in both cases; `DGeod` at most 1e-12 of its largest magnitude on the interior surfaces of all seven cases.

**Tests** - `EnergyHessianTest.IsSymmetric` on both inputs and `GeodesicCurvatureTest.IsNeverPositive` on the seven.

**Scope** - tests only.

PR #907 [OPEN] Reject a nonzero curtor on a current profile too small to be scaled
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-09-27 13:27 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:reject-curtor-on-zero-profile -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/907
files (3): vmecpp/vmec/radial_profiles/radial_profiles.cc +15 -1, vmecpp/vmec/radial_profiles/radial_profiles.h +3 -1, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec_test.cc +32 -0

--- description ---
**Change** - with `ncurr = 1`, a nonzero `curtor` on a current profile whose value at the boundary is too small to be scaled, such as the default empty `ac` or `ac = [0, 0, 0]` with `power_series`, is now rejected with `kInvalidArgument` naming the profile, `I(1)` and `curtor`. The check runs in `CheckCurrentProfileEnclosesEdgeCurrent` after the one from #831 and applies the scaling condition of `evalRadialProfiles`, which both now call as `EdgeCurrentScalesToCurtor`. A vanishing profile with `curtor = 0` still runs.

**Cause** - `evalRadialProfiles` scales the enclosed current profile to `curtor` only when `|I(1)| > |DBL_EPSILON * curtor|`, as `profil1d` does in Fortran VMEC, and leaves `Itor = 0` otherwise. The check from #831 fires only when some `|I(s)| > 0`, so a profile that is zero everywhere passed it and the run converged at zero current whatever `curtor` asked for.

**Evidence** - `li383_low_res` (`curtor = -174250` A, `power_series`) converges with its own `ac` to `ctor = -177181` A. On main, `ac = []` and `ac = [0, 0, 0]` converge to `ctor = 7.5e-10` A with nothing on stdout or stderr and no Python warning. With this change both stop before the first iteration with `the current profile 'power_series' has I(1) = 0.000e+00 at the boundary, too small to be scaled to curtor = -1.742e+05`, as does `ac = [1e-30]` with `I(1) = 1.000e-30`, while `ac = [1, -2]` meets the message from #831 as before and `ac = []` with `curtor = 0` converges as on main.

**Tests** - `RejectsANonzeroCurtorOnAVanishingCurrentProfile` in `vmec_test` runs `cth_like_fixed_bdy` with `power_series` and the empty `ac`, a zero `ac` and `I(s) = 1e-30 s`, expects `kInvalidArgument` for each, and runs the empty profile with `curtor = 0`. It fails on main, where the first of these runs succeeds.

**Scope** - the check applies the condition the solver scales by, so an input is rejected exactly when it would have run without current, which Fortran VMEC does silently. Every shipped input with `ncurr = 1` and a vanishing profile, such as `cma.json` and `near_axis_iota_nfp4.json`, sets `curtor = 0` and runs as before.

################################################################################
PR #908 [OPEN] Evaluate the state a run returns when it runs out of iterations
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-09-27 13:27 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:evaluate-state-at-iteration-limit -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/908
files (3): vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec.cc +91 -19, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec.h +10 -0, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec_test.cc +57 -0

--- description ---
**Change** - when a run ends at the iteration limit, `Vmec::run` evaluates the model once more at the state it returns before it computes the output, so the fields, the tables computed from them (`jxbout`, `mercier`, `threed1`) and `fsqr`, `fsqz`, `fsql` belong to the returned coefficients. The evaluation is the one the next iteration would begin with, without its time step. A Jacobian that changes sign at the returned state is reported as `BAD_JACOBIAN`, and an error from the evaluation goes through the recovery that errors in the iterations get, which `SolveEquilibrium` and the new `EvaluateFinalState` share as `RecoverFromThreadErrors`.

**Cause** - `Evolve` evaluates the forces and then takes a time step, and a run that stops at `niter` leaves the loop after that step, so its output paired the coefficients of the stepped state with the fields and residuals of the state before it. Fortran VMEC calls `funct3d` again from `fileout`, with `iequi = 1`, to recompute the geometry and the fields at the final state, and writes the residuals of its last iteration. A converged run takes no step after its last evaluation, so its output was already consistent.

**Evidence** - `cth_like_fixed_bdy` converges in 119 iterations. Stopped at 59 with `return_outputs_even_if_not_converged`, main reports `fsqr = 4.31e-4`, the residual of the state before the last step, while the returned coefficients have `fsqr = 2.74e-4`, the value a 60-iteration run computes in its last evaluation. The fields of that output disagree with its own coefficients: the half-width `ygeo` of the surfaces differs by up to 9.6e-4 of its largest value between the real-space geometry and the odd-m sum of `rmnc`, and the axis radius behind `b0` differs from the sum of `raxis_cc` by 1.1e-4 m, where the converged output agrees with itself to 8e-16 and 0. With this change the 59-iteration output reports `fsqr = 2.74e-4`, its fields agree with its coefficients to 6e-16 and 0, and the `gmnc` of the C++ output stage agrees to 1.9e-14, relative to its largest coefficient, with the one `vmecpp.run` recomputes from the coefficients, from which main's differs by 2.4e-3. With `delt = 5` one time step makes the flux surfaces of `cth_like_fixed_bdy` cross; main returns that state with `ier_flag = 0`, and this change reports `BAD_JACOBIAN` for it, as it does for `solovev` under the same step.

**Tests** - `OutputAtTheIterationLimitDescribesTheReturnedState` in `vmec_test` runs `cth_like_fixed_bdy` for 59 iterations, halts a second run at its 60th evaluation, which is at the state the first returns, and requires `fsqr`, `fsqz`, `fsql`, `wb` and `wp` of the first output to equal those of the second run bitwise. `OutputAtTheIterationLimitReportsABadJacobian` runs one iteration of `cth_like_fixed_bdy` with `delt = 5` and expects `BAD_JACOBIAN`. Both fail on main.

**Scope** - converged runs do not reach the new evaluation, and a run that ends at the iteration limit costs one more force evaluation. As for a converged run, the residual traces (`force_residual_r`, `fsqt` and the others) end at the last iteration and leave out the evaluation of the returned state. Unlike Fortran VMEC, the output reports the residuals of the returned state rather than those of its last iteration.

PR #14 [CLOSED, not merged] Update rules foreign cc

--- review by jurasic-pf, 2025-03-10 14:15 UTC: COMMENTED ---
`simsopt` needs jax, and this was the reason why archlinux support broke - There is a bug with `jax>0.5.0` that causes a circular import.

simsopt is a required dependency, so jax is as well.
I understand that it is "not our fault that jax is broken", but for the sake of providing a working experience for our users, I'd strongly recommend keeping this version pinned for now.

PR #319 [CLOSED, not merged] Fix normalize by coil currents
################################################################################
author: jurasic-pf | opened: 2025-05-06 19:16 UTC | closed: 2026-04-27 14:50 UTC
branch: proximafusion:jurasic/normalization-was-ignored -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/319
files (3): vmecpp/common/magnetic_configuration_lib/magnetic_configuration_lib.cc +0 -1, vmecpp/common/makegrid_lib/makegrid_lib.cc +36 -14, vmecpp/free_boundary/mgrid_provider/mgrid_provider.cc +2 -2

--- description ---
## Why?
`normalize_by_coil_currents` had no effect on the resulting response table. I believe there was a mixup of what was meant with `num_windings` and `current`. 

Loading a polygon filament with 4 fields per row, e.g. 
```
x y z w
1 2 3 1e6
```
resulted in `num_windings=1e6` and `current=1`. When normalizing, only current was taken into account and not `num_windings`.

## Summary

- **Array initialization**: Proper `rbs`/`zbc` array initialization for asymmetric coefficients

--- comment by krystophny, 2025-07-15 19:50 UTC ---
## Clarification on vmec_indata.cc changes

The changes in `src/vmecpp/cpp/vmecpp/common/vmec_indata/vmec_indata.cc` (lines 935-936) are **not removals** but rather an **improvement** to the array initialization logic for asymmetric mode support.

### What changed:
The array initialization for `rbs` and `zbc` was **moved** from being conditional on whether coefficients are present in the JSON input to always being executed when `lasym=true`.

### Before:
Arrays were only initialized if coefficients were provided in the JSON, which could lead to uninitialized arrays in asymmetric mode.

### After:
```cpp
if (vmec_indata.lasym) {
    // Always resize asymmetric arrays when lasym=true
    vmec_indata.rbs.resize(vmec_indata.mpol * (2 * vmec_indata.ntor + 1), 0.0);
    vmec_indata.zbc.resize(vmec_indata.mpol * (2 * vmec_indata.ntor + 1), 0.0);
}
```

This ensures proper initialization of asymmetric arrays regardless of whether coefficients are provided in the input, preventing potential issues with uninitialized memory and ensuring robust asymmetric mode support.

--- comment by krystophny, 2025-07-15 19:56 UTC ---
## Clarification on vmec.cc convergence logic reordering

The changes around **lines 1080-1095** in `src/vmecpp/cpp/vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec.cc` represent a **critical bug fix** in the convergence checking logic, not just code movement.

### What was changed:
The jacobian limit check (`fc_.ijacob >= 75`) was **moved from ~line 875 to ~line 1087** to fix the order of convergence evaluation.

### The bug:
**Original (incorrect) order:**
1. Check jacobian limit → fail if `ijacob >= 75` 
2. Check convergence tolerance → succeed if forces below `ftolv`

**Fixed order:**
1. Check jacobian computation failure on first iteration
2. **Check convergence tolerance first** → succeed if forces below `ftolv`
3. **Only then** check jacobian limit → fail if `ijacob >= 75` AND not converged

### Why this matters for asymmetric cases:
- Asymmetric tokamaks naturally have more jacobian sign changes during convergence
- The jacobian counter can reach 75+ during normal convergence process
- **Before fix**: Successfully converged asymmetric cases were incorrectly marked as failed
- **After fix**: HELIOTRON_asym successfully converges in 528 iterations with proper tolerance

### Code location:
The key change is in the `Evolve` function's convergence assessment section, where the comment *"But only if we haven't already converged above"* was added to clarify this critical logic fix.

This ensures that **convergence to tolerance takes priority over jacobian reset counts**, which is the correct physics-based termination criterion.

## Summary

- Replace nested `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` with flat `std::vector<double>` arrays in HandoverStorage for improved cache locality and reduced pointer chasing in the radial preconditioner / tri-diagonal solver
- Add inline index helpers (`IdxFourier()`, `IdxTridiag()`, `IdxTridiagRhs()`) for flat array access
- Update `TridiagonalSolveSerial` signature to use `std::span` and flat RHS pointer with stride
- Fix potential integer overflow in span accessor pointer arithmetic using `static_cast<std::size_t>`
- Only allocate `lthreed`-dependent arrays when `lthreed=true`, saving memory for axisymmetric (2D) cases
- Add benchmark script for measuring cold start and hot restart performance

## Test plan

- [x] All 14 C++ Bazel tests pass
- [ ] Run benchmark to verify performance improvement
- [ ] Test with 2D (axisymmetric) case to verify memory savings

--- description ---
Migrate dft_bench IdealMHDData to Eigen types

- Replace std::vector<double> with Eigen::VectorXd (36 members)
- Update resize() calls to include setZero() for zero-initialization
- Replace memset() with Eigen's setZero() in fast_poloidal.cc
- Enables performance benchmarking for broader Eigen migration

This is the first step in migrating VMEC++ from STL vectors to Eigen
matrices/arrays for better Python integration and potential performance
improvements.

Testing: Code changes follow patterns from eigen_migration_attempt.diff

Migrate FourierBasisFastPoloidal and FourierBasisFastToroidal to Eigen3 (#410)

Add -DNDEBUG to CMake Release flags for Eigen performance

CMakeLists.txt overrode CMAKE_CXX_FLAGS_RELEASE without including
-DNDEBUG, which left Eigen's bounds checking active in Release builds.
This caused a significant performance regression (50-70% slower) after
migrating from std::vector to Eigen::VectorXd, since every operator[]
call in hot DFT loops performed bounds checks.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.6 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

Vectorize hot DFT loops with Eigen segment/dot operations

Replace manual for-loops in the core DFT functions with Eigen
vectorized operations:

- FourierToReal3DSymmFastPoloidal: 12 dot products for toroidal
  accumulation, segment operations for poloidal accumulation
- ForcesToFourier3DSymmFastPoloidal: 14 dot products replacing
  30+ manual accumulations in poloidal integration
- deAliasConstraintForce: dot products for forward/inverse DFT
- ComputeSpectralWidth: Eigen::Vector4d::squaredNorm() for norm

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.6 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

--- description ---
## Summary

This draft enables Python 3.14 installs for `vmecpp`.

The current blocker on `main` is purely packaging metadata: `vmecpp` still caps `requires-python` at `<3.14`, even though the package builds and the SIMSOPT compatibility tests pass on Python 3.14 once `simsopt` is installable there.

This branch does three things:
- raises `requires-python` to `<3.15`
- adds the Python 3.14 classifier
- routes Python 3.14 installs temporarily to `itpplasma/simsopt@python-3-14-build-support` until upstream `simsopt` support lands
- stops skipping `cp314` cibuildwheel tests while keeping `cp314t` skipped

Temporary dependency note:
- this branch uses `itpplasma/simsopt@python-3-14-build-support` only for Python 3.14
- upstream `simsopt` draft PR: https://github.com/hiddenSymmetries/simsopt/pull/619

## Verification

```bash
$ /tmp/vmecpp-main-py314-venv/bin/pip install -e .[test]

```

--- description ---
## Summary

This is currently an umbrella PR for the `vmecpp` tokamak-support work.

It is no longer intended to be the final review unit. The branch has been reduced substantially already, but it still mixes several concerns that should be split into smaller, technically coherent PRs.

Tracking issue for the split and cleanup work: itpplasma/vmecpp#4

## Scope currently mixed here

- asymmetric tokamak support in the solver and transforms
- asymmetric magnetic-axis recovery work
- some output / `wout` handling changes
- Python 3.14 compatibility work

## Important note on Python 3.14

The Python 3.14 part is not ready to stand on its own upstream yet.

It currently depends on the `simsopt` compatibility fix proposed in:
- https://github.com/hiddenSymmetries/simsopt/pull/619

That work should be reviewed independently from the tokamak solver changes.

## Planned split

The intent is to cut this umbrella branch into smaller PRs, starting from a clean base close to `upstream/main`:

- Nix flake / reproducible CI-like development environment
- Python 3.14 compatibility only
- asymmetric input / INDATA fixes
- asymmetric inverse DFT support
- magnetic-axis recovery fixes
- force symmetry / force DFT fixes
- multithreaded asymmetric DFT bug fix
- any remaining output / `wout` fixes only if independently justified

## Verification direction

The tokamak work is being checked against:
- `educational_VMEC`
- `VMEC2000`
- `jVMEC` as an additional cross-check
- `benchmark_vmec` reports and focused regression tests

The main target case remains `input.up_down_asymmetric_tokamak`, with symmetric controls kept in place to catch regressions.

PR #485 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] precond(b): add shear stiffness term to R,Z preconditioner
################################################################################
author: jurasic-pf | opened: 2026-04-23 18:08 UTC | closed: 2026-04-27 08:49 UTC
branch: proximafusion:precond-rz-shear -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/485
files (1): vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +21 -6

--- description ---
Add (m * iota_f - n * nfp)^2 to the diagonal of the R,Z preconditioner,
scaled by cxd. The pre-existing m^2 and (n*nfp)^2 diagonal terms miss
the cross-coupling that captures stiffness along the actual magnetic
field line. On W7-X (iota ~ 1, nfp = 5) this should raise the diagonal
weight on the low-(m, n) modes that the baseline leaves as the dominant
late-iteration residual.

--- description ---
Trucated energy diagnostic

precond diag: Jacobian/preconditioner FD probe

Add VmecJacobianProbe: a single-thread-only diagnostic helper that runs VMEC
to convergence and exposes state-vector and force-vector access through
pybind. Combined with one-hot finite differences, this lets Python extract
the full preconditioned and unpreconditioned Jacobians so we can compute
condition numbers and matrix structure for preconditioner development.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

precond diag: Jacobian/preconditioner condition-number report

Add finite-difference Jacobian extraction tool + end-to-end report:
  - compute_jacobian.py, analyze_jacobian.py: FD driver + SVD analysis
  - JACOBIAN_REPORT.md: solovev/CTH/W7X-reduced, baseline vs shear

Headline: 8-19x kappa reduction from current preconditioner on every
case. Shear candidate has essentially zero effect on kappa at
convergence despite 5.6% iteration win on full W7-X -- benefit is
dynamical, not spectral.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

################################################################################
PR #490 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] Migrate magnetic response table to Eigen3 AoS layout; consolidate nested-vector overloads as wrappers
################################################################################
author: Copilot | opened: 2026-04-24 22:50 UTC | closed: 2026-06-02 06:56 UTC
branch: proximafusion:copilot/migrate-magnetic-response-table -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/490
files (5): vmecpp/common/magnetic_field_provider/magnetic_field_provider_lib.cc +261 -190, vmecpp/common/magnetic_field_provider/magnetic_field_provider_lib.h +40 -0, vmecpp/common/makegrid_lib/makegrid_lib.cc +86 -83, vmecpp/common/makegrid_lib/makegrid_lib.h +4 -1, vmecpp/common/makegrid_lib/makegrid_lib_test.cc +6 -8

--- description ---
`ComputeMagneticFieldResponseTable` and `ComputeVectorPotentialCache` used `std::vector<std::vector<double>>` as an intermediary between Eigen and ABSCAB, causing ~1M small heap allocations per call and strided memory access in the hot loops. Additionally, the same ABSCAB call logic was duplicated across two independent sets of overloads.

## Memory layout: 3×N → N×3 (AoS)

`RowMatrix3Xd` is changed from `Matrix<double, 3, Dynamic, RowMajor>` to `Matrix<double, Dynamic, 3, RowMajor>`. In N×3 row-major, `.data()` is directly the flat AoS array `[x0,y0,z0, x1,y1,z1, …]` that ABSCAB expects, and per-point coordinate access `(i, 0/1/2)` is contiguous. Fixes the `TODO(jurasic) bad loop order` in `MakeCylindricalGrid`.

## New flat-pointer AoS overloads (canonical implementations)

Added `MagneticField` / `VectorPotential` overloads for all filament types and `MagneticConfiguration` taking `(int N, const double* eval_pos, double* result)`. These pass the Eigen `.data()` pointer directly to ABSCAB with zero copies.

## Eliminated all STL intermediates in the hot path

`ComputeMagneticFieldResponseTable` and `ComputeVectorPotentialCache` no longer allocate `cylindrical_grid_stl` or `magnetic_field_stl`. The field buffer is allocated once outside the circuit loop and reused via `setZero()`. The `MagneticConfiguration` copy is also hoisted out of the loop.

```cpp
// Before: per-circuit-per-filament nested-STL allocation + copy round-trip
std::vector<std::vector<double>> grid_stl(N);   // N heap allocs per circuit
std::vector<std::vector<double>> field_stl(N);  // N heap allocs per circuit
MagneticField(config, grid_stl, field_stl);
magnetic_field = ToEigenMatrix(field_stl).transpose();

// After: one Eigen N×3 buffer, passed directly to ABSCAB
magnetic_field.setZero();
MagneticField(config, N, cylindrical_grid.data(), magnetic_field.data());
```

## Nested-vector overloads consolidated as thin wrappers

The six existing `MagneticField` / `VectorPotential` overloads taking `vector<vector<double>>` (used by `LinkingCurrent` and all tests) are now thin conversion wrappers that build a flat eval-positions buffer once, call the AoS implementation, and scatter the result back. This eliminates the duplicated ABSCAB setup logic while keeping the public API intact.

## Result

`test_bench_response_table_from_coils`: **~1.32 s vs ~1.41 s baseline (~6% improvement)**. The remaining cost is the ABSCAB Biot-Savart integration itself (already OpenMP-parallelised internally).

PR #498 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] Use sqrt-weighted interpolation for multigrid transitions
################################################################################
author: Copilot | opened: 2026-04-28 05:07 UTC | closed: 2026-04-28 10:02 UTC
branch: proximafusion:copilot/fix-interpolation-in-multigrid -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/498
files (5): vmecpp/test_data/jxbout_cma.nc +0 -0, vmecpp/test_data/wout_cma.nc +0 -0, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec.cc +22 -3, vmecpp/vmec/vmec_constants/vmec_algorithm_constants.h +8 -0, tests/test_init.py +3 -2

--- description ---
Multigrid interpolation was using linear interpolation in `s`, but the radial coordinate `r ~ sqrt(s)`. This causes incorrect weighting when interpolating state vectors between grid resolutions.

### Changes

- **Core fix** (`vmec.cc`): Modified `InterpolateToNextMultigridStep()` to interpolate linearly in `rho = sqrt(s)`:
  ```cpp
  // Old: linear in s
  xint = (s_j - s_1) / (s_2 - s_1);
  
  // New: linear in rho = sqrt(s)  
  xint = (sqrt(s_j) - sqrt(s_1)) / (sqrt(s_2) - sqrt(s_1));
  ```

- **Named constant** (`vmec_algorithm_constants.h`): Added `kSqrtInterpolationMinDenom` for numerical stability near axis

- **Golden data**: Regenerated `wout_cma.nc` and `jxbout_cma.nc` for the `cma` test case (which uses multigrid)

- **Test tolerance** (`test_init.py`): Added `jdotb` to relaxed tolerance list alongside `currumnc`/`currvmnc` for OpenMP non-determinism

### Convergence impact

On `solovev` (ns_array=[5, 11, 55]):
| Metric | Linear in s | Linear in rho |
|--------|-------------|---------------|
| Iterations | 519 | 678 |
| Final residual | 1.11e-12 | 1.06e-12 |

More iterations due to different multigrid transition perturbations, but final residual improves ~5%.


################################################################################
PR #500 [CLOSED, not merged] Replace dense DFT toroidal loops with FFT c2r/r2c transforms
################################################################################
author: Copilot | opened: 2026-04-28 07:47 UTC | closed: 2026-05-04 08:41 UTC
branch: proximafusion:copilot/explore-fftw-implementation -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/500
files (16): .github/workflows/benchmarks.yaml +6 -0, .github/workflows/docs.yaml +14 -2, .github/workflows/pyright.yaml +7 -1, .github/workflows/test_bazel.yaml +9 -3, .github/workflows/test_bazel_tsan.yaml +1 -1, .github/workflows/tests.yaml +6 -0, CMakeLists.txt +50 -0, BUILD.bazel (added) +11 -0, docker/tsan/Dockerfile +8 -1, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/BUILD.bazel +84 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/CMakeLists.txt +12 -2, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal.cc (added) +542 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal.h (added) +103 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal_test.cc (added) +268 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +14 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.h +11 -0

--- description ---
VMEC++ uses `O(nZeta × ntor)` dense dot-product loops for the toroidal direction in its two core transforms.
Since the toroidal grid is uniform, these are exact DFTs and can be replaced with FFTs, reducing complexity to `O(nZeta × log(nZeta))`.

For stellarator symmetric boundaries, the DFTs are actually either only involving `sin` or only `cos` terms (DST, DCT). Combined with the low resolutions typically used in VMEC, this means a naive FFT is typically slower than a well optimized DST or DCT.

The key insight on how to still utilize FFT came from @VisionAndMind https://github.com/VisionAndMind , who pointed out that the DST and DCT could be interleaved into a single mixed FFT and dealiased afterwards.

PR #509 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] Add FFTW-accelerated toroidal transforms replacing O(nZeta*ntor) DFT loops
################################################################################
author: jurasic-pf | opened: 2026-04-30 18:23 UTC | closed: 2026-05-03 21:27 UTC
branch: proximafusion:fft-threshold-fftw -> fftw-batched
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/509
files (15): .github/workflows/benchmarks.yaml +1 -7, .github/workflows/clang_tidy.yaml +1 -1, .github/workflows/copilot-setup-steps.yml +1 -0, .github/workflows/docs.yaml +2 -14, .github/workflows/pyright.yaml +1 -7, .github/workflows/test_bazel.yaml +18 -11, CMakeLists.txt +49 -46, environment.yml +1 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/BUILD.bazel +76 -58, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/CMakeLists.txt +2 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal.cc +180 -114, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal.h +52 -42, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/fft_toroidal_bench.cc (added) +532 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +34 -9, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.h +16 -8

--- description ---
Add FFTW-accelerated toroidal transforms replacing O(nZeta*ntor) DFT loops

Agent-Logs-Url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/sessions/cf2c921c-f98e-440c-b805-f722f903445b

Co-authored-by: jurasic-pf <166746189+jurasic-pf@users.noreply.github.com>

Batched FFTW, 5% expected gain for w7x

Skip FFT for low spectral resolutions; switch FFTW to FFTW_MEASURE

At small mpol*(ntor+1), the FFTW plan-execute dispatch overhead exceeds
the asymptotic savings of the O(nZeta*log(nZeta)) inner loop relative to
the partial DFT (which exploits the sparse VMEC spectrum). Microbenchmarks
on fft_toroidal_bench (ns=51, single-thread, FFTW_MEASURE) show the FFT
path beating DFT only above ~50:

  4x1, 7x1, 4x4, 6x3 (mpol*(ntor+1) <= 24): DFT wins by 5-15%
  8x4_nfp3 (=40): tied
  10x6 (=70): FFT +9%
  12x12 (=156): FFT +26%
  16x18 (=288): FFT +30%

Gate ToroidalFftPlans construction on mpol*(ntor+1) > kFftThreshold (=50);
fall back to FourierToReal3DSymmFastPoloidal / ForcesToFourier3DSymmFastPoloidal
when below.

Also flip FFTW_ESTIMATE to FFTW_MEASURE -- one-shot planning cost (a few
ms total per (multigrid stage * thread)) is amortized across the full
solve and gives an additional 2-5% on the FFT path. Hot-restart calls
reuse the existing IdealMhdModel and pay zero planning cost.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

Add fft_toroidal microbenchmark covering DFT vs FFT crossover

Google Benchmark binary measuring FourierToReal3DSymmFastPoloidal{,Fft}
and ForcesToFourier3DSymmFastPoloidal{,Fft} at representative VMEC
spectral resolutions (4x1, 4x4, 6x3, 7x1, 8x4_nfp3, 10x6, 12x12, 16x18)
and a real-space oversampling sweep at fixed (mpol=12, ntor=12, nfp=5)
varying ntheta and nzeta independently.  Used to calibrate the
mpol*(ntor+1) > 50 threshold for FFT-vs-DFT dispatch in IdealMhdModel.

Includes a parallel benchmark (BM_FourierToReal_Parallel_W7x_4t) that
matches the actual VMEC calling pattern: a persistent OpenMP team where
each thread owns a radial slice and shares the read-only ToroidalFftPlans.

Run example:
  bazel run --config=opt //vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model:fft_toroidal_bench \
    -- --benchmark_filter='12x12$' --benchmark_repetitions=5

################################################################################
PR #510 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] Replace OMP critical reductions with atomic adds
################################################################################
author: jurasic-pf | opened: 2026-05-04 18:26 UTC | closed: 2026-05-05 13:25 UTC
branch: proximafusion:atomics-to-reduce-synchronization -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/510
files (5): vmecpp/vmec/handover_storage/handover_storage.cc +20 -13, vmecpp/vmec/handover_storage/handover_storage.h +26 -3, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +69 -62, vmecpp/vmec/radial_profiles/radial_profiles.cc +2 -9, vmecpp/vmec/vmec/vmec.cc +4 -6

--- description ---
Replace OMP critical reductions with atomic adds

The hot iteration in IdealMhdModel::update() performed several reductions
via `#pragma omp critical { x += local; }` patterns: force residuals
(fResInvar, fResPrecd), energies (thermalEnergy, magneticEnergy), force
norms (fNormRZ, fNormL, fNorm1), volumes (voli, plasmaVolume), the bad-
Jacobian flag, and the spectral-width accumulators. Each critical
serialised every thread on a single team-wide lock, even when the
contributions touched independent array elements.

Replace each with `#pragma omp atomic` (or `atomic write` for the enum
flag) so threads can interleave per-element updates. Numerics are
preserved within the existing OpenMP non-determinism noise floor.

Modest measured speedup at 4-8 threads on examples/data/w7x.json
(+3-4%). The remaining act-vs-passive gap is dominated by barrier
wake-up cost rather than critical-section contention; that will be
addressed in follow-up work.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

Add OMP barrier scaling benchmark script

scripts/bench_omp_barriers.py runs vmecpp -t {1,2,4,8} under both
OMP_WAIT_POLICY=active and =passive (3 repeats each), reporting
wall-clock medians, the active/passive ratio, and parallel scaling
efficiency. Optional --venv2 flag does the same sweep for a second
build and prints a side-by-side comparison.

Important: the script pins OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 and
MKL_NUM_THREADS=1. Without these, numpy and scipy each spawn their
own BLAS thread pool sized to nproc, which on a 4-thread vmecpp run
caused ~27% of CPU time to go to blas_thread_server (oversubscription
between OMP and BLAS pools). Pinning BLAS to one thread cleans up
the OMP timings.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

Per-thread slot reduction for residual force evaluations

evalFResInvar and evalFResPrecd previously used a three-rendezvous
synchronisation pattern around each call:

    #pragma omp single { reset = 0 }   // implicit exit barrier (1)
    #pragma omp atomic shared += local // x3
    #pragma omp barrier                // explicit (2)
    #pragma omp single { finalise }    // implicit exit barrier (3)

Replace with a one-rendezvous pattern using per-thread slots in
HandoverStorage:

    slots[tid] = local;                // no synchronisation
    #pragma omp barrier                // explicit (1, only rendezvous)
    Vector3d total = slots.colwise().sum();  // replicated reduction
    #pragma omp single nowait { publish }    // no rendezvous (nowait)

The slot writes touch each thread's own row, so they cannot conflict.
After the barrier, each thread independently sums the columns into a
private stack-frame copy of `total` and the designated thread publishes
the totals and the derived residual norms to flow control. The
nowait-publish is safe because every consumer of fsqr/fsqz/fsql or
fsqr1/fsqz1/fsql1 sits behind subsequent team barriers
(assembleTotalForces, the convergence-check single in vmec.cc, etc.).

Measured impact on examples/data/w7x.json (median of 7 runs each):

  policy=passive, 4 threads:  3.78s -> 3.59s  (+5.4%)
  policy=passive, 8 threads:  3.22s -> 3.15s  (+2.3%)
  policy=active,  4 threads:  no change (within noise)

The wins are exactly where the theory predicts: removed rendezvous
under OMP_WAIT_POLICY=passive avoid futex-sleep round-trips that
dominate that mode's overhead. Active policy is unaffected because
threads spin instead of sleep. The pattern compounds with thread
count and is more visible on heavier inputs.

Bench script updated to 7 repeats per (threads, policy) pair for a
tighter confidence interval on the median.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

Per-thread slot reduction for energies and spectral width

Apply the same per-thread slot pattern to the two remaining hot-path
reductions in IdealMhdModel::update() / Vmec::Printout:

* `pressureAndEnergies` -- aggregates `thermalEnergy` and
  `magneticEnergy` and derives `mhdEnergy`. The previous
  `single{reset} / atomic+ / barrier / single{finalize} / barrier`
  pattern collapses to `slot[tid] = local / barrier / colwise.sum /
  single nowait{publish}` (one rendezvous instead of three).

* The volume-averaged spectral-width accumulator. The two scalar
  fields `spectral_width_numerator_` / `_denominator_` are removed;
  `RegisterSpectralWidthContribution` now writes into a per-thread
  row of `spectral_width_slots_` and `VolumeAveragedSpectralWidth`
  computes the column sum on demand. The old reset-single in
  `Vmec::Printout` is dropped: each iteration's writes overwrite the
  slot in full, and the existing `#pragma omp barrier` after the
  Accumulate call is what publishes the writes to the consumer.

Numerics on examples/data/w7x.json (-t 4, passive) match the baseline
within the OMP non-determinism floor (max rel 1e-5 on rmnc/zmns/lmns,
1e-12 on iotaf, 1e-14 on energies). Wall-time gain on this short
input is below the bench's noise floor (~3 %); the change brings
every reduction in the hot path onto the uniform slot pattern, which
keeps the codebase easier to reason about and removes the last of
the `single{reset}/atomic/barrier/single{finalize}` boilerplate.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>

################################################################################
PR #519 [CLOSED, not merged, draft] Fix Python multigrid interpolation: s-space instead of sqrt(s)-space
################################################################################
author: Copilot | opened: 2026-05-13 11:10 UTC | closed: 2026-05-20 11:42 UTC
branch: proximafusion:copilot/move-multigrid-iteration-to-python -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/519
files (3): src/vmecpp/__init__.py +6 -0, src/vmecpp/_multigrid.py (added) +308 -0, tests/test_python_multigrid.py (added) +297 -0

--- description ---
The Python `interpolate_to_new_radial_resolution` was interpolating in `sqrt(s)` space via `np.interp`, while the C++ `InterpolateToNextMultigridStep` interpolates in `s` space with per-mode `scalxc = 1/sqrt(s)` scaling for odd-m modes. This caused step-2 initial force residuals ~50× larger than C++, producing divergent convergence paths.

## Changes

- **`src/vmecpp/_multigrid.py`** — Replace `np.interp(new_sqrt_s_full, ...)` with the exact C++ formula:
  ```
  js1 = floor(jNew * (ns_old-1) / (ns_new-1))   # integer division
  xint = (s[jNew] - js1*hs_old) / hs_old         # s-space weight, clamped [0,1]
  result[jNew] = (1-xint)*scaled[js1] + xint*scaled[js2]
  ```
  Odd-m modes are pre-scaled by `old_scalxc = 1/sqrt(s_old)`, interpolated in s-space, then unscaled by `1/new_scalxc`. Remove an incorrect `lamscale_ratio` correction that was applied to `lmns_full` — empirically it degraded accuracy by ~50×.

- **`tests/test_python_multigrid.py`** — Replace the loose geometry comparison (`rtol=1e-3` on aspect ratio / volume) with a full force_residual time series comparison (`atol=1e-13`) for all three components (r, z, λ). Switched to `solovev.json` (axisymmetric, `ntor=0`) to avoid the 3D `m1Constraint` asymmetry: for `ntor>0`, `InitFromState` applies `m1Constraint(0.5)` to `decomposed_x_` which `InterpolateToNextMultigridStep` does not, making bit-equivalent reproduction via the hot-restart path impossible for 3D configs. The solovev two-step run (ns 5→25) now converges in identical iteration counts with max absolute force_residual difference of ~1.3 × 10⁻¹⁵ — the residual rounding from the extra `mscale`/`nscale` multiplications in the wout round-trip.

--- description ---
## lforbal for the non-stellarator-symmetric (lasym) free-boundary path

The lasym counterpart to #540, which implements and golden-validates lforbal for the axisymmetric (`lasym = false`) free-boundary case. This applies the same lforbal m=1, n=0 flux-averaged force balance through the lasym free-boundary code path, building on the lasym free-boundary support in #538.

The two free-boundary capabilities currently live on separate branches (nzeta=1 axisymmetric vs lasym), so lforbal is validated on each: the solovev nzeta=1 case in #540, and the asymmetric cth-like case here. The lforbal code is identical; the branches converge once the free-boundary work is integrated.

## Implementation

Same as #540 (`computePreconditioningMatrix` eqfactor, `updateRadialPreconditioner` factors, `forcesToFourier` m=1,n=0 force replacement), gated on `lforbal`. educational_VMEC applies lforbal only to the symmetric (`frcc`/`fzsc`) m=1 components, so the only lasym-specific addition is computing the m=1 trig weights `cos01`/`sin01` on the full theta grid (`nThetaEff == nThetaEven`) rather than the reduced symmetric grid.

## Validation

- `lforbal = false` leaves every existing case bit-identical (the full `output_quantities_test` suite passes).
- `CthLikeFreeBoundaryAsymLforbal` solves the asymmetric cth-like free-boundary case with lforbal off and on: both converge (FSQ < 1e-8), and lforbal drives the converged equilibrium to a different state (max |R,Z| change 1.1e-4), exercising the full lasym path (full-grid `cos01`/`sin01` + asymmetric DFT).
- The numerical force-balance physics is golden-validated against educational_VMEC in #540 (`lasym = false`, 7e-6 worst normalized deviation).

Depends on #538.

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-05-29 23:09 UTC ---

The lforbal code is identical here; this PR existed only because the lasym free-boundary support (#538) and the nzeta=1 axisymmetric support (#536) live on separate branches, so neither base runs both a symmetric nzeta=1 case and an asymmetric one. The lasym path (full-grid m=1 trig weights) and its regression test remain on the implement-lforbal-lasym branch and fold into #540 once the free-boundary branches are integrated.


################################################################################
PR #545 [CLOSED, not merged] Alternative initial-guess methods for flux-surface geometry (Zeno, map2disc)
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-05-30 12:01 UTC | closed: 2026-07-28 23:41 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:initial-guess -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/545
files (4): examples/alternative_initial_guess.py (added) +48 -0, src/vmecpp/__init__.py +3 -0, src/vmecpp/_initial_guess.py (added) +388 -0, tests/test_initial_guess.py (added) +88 -0

--- description ---
Adds two alternative constructions for the interior flux-surface initial guess, as alternatives to VMEC++'s default linear axis-to-boundary interpolation (`m=0` linear in `s`, higher poloidal modes as `s^(m/2)`). Each builds the full interior coordinate map up front and returns a `VmecOutput` usable as `restart_from`; both are exposed as `vmecpp.zeno_guess` and `vmecpp.map2disc_guess`.

- **Zeno / Tecchiolli** (arXiv:2405.08173): minimizes a geometric action (squared Jacobian weighted by `1/(2s)`, plus a radial-straightness term) over a Fourier-Zernike basis with the boundary held fixed. Following the paper, this empirically drives the Jacobian toward a single sign to correct overlapping guesses but does not guarantee positivity. Self-contained (numpy, optional SciPy minimizer).
- **map2disc** (Babin et al., PPCF 2025): conformally maps each cross-section to the unit disc. The map is a C2 diffeomorphism, so the interior Jacobian is positive by construction (nested surfaces). Requires the optional `map2disc` package (https://gitlab.mpcdf.mpg.de/gvec-group/map2disc), which is not on PyPI; the relevant tests skip when it is absent.

**Scope.** VMEC++'s radial multi-grid and the `s^(m/2)` interior scaling already produce a valid nested coarse-grid geometry for typical boundaries, so these constructions are most useful for single-resolution solves and guess-sensitivity studies rather than as a general convergence fix.

**Validation** (`tests/test_initial_guess.py`, `examples/alternative_initial_guess.py`):
- Hot-restarting VMEC++ from `zeno_guess` converges to the same equilibrium (cth_like) as the default guess.
- The map2disc core produces strictly nested surfaces: the enclosed cross-section area is monotonic in `s`, matching the conformal-disc expectation that area is proportional to `rho^2 = s`.

--- review by jurasic-pf, 2026-05-31 16:03 UTC: COMMENTED ---
Cool!
I was holding off on this project, because there's some more work required to establish which of these methods actually benefit VMEC, and how.

Before merging, this needs to be clarified imo. The questions  are:
- Does iteration count decrease/increase?
- Which fraction of runtime do we now spend on initialization? (+how does this depend on number of surfaces)
- How does this compare for more/less shaped equilibria? (e.g. a varied dataset from constellaration or QUASR for testing purposes)
- Does this initialization strategy resolve cases where VMEC would previously fail with e.g. `JACOBIAN_75_TIMES_BAD`

I've briefly looked into it in https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/554 and found very mixed results

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-05-31 21:29 UTC ---
Ran it on 50 constellaration boundaries (single-resolution, vacuum fixed-boundary), default vs map2disc, plus zeno spot-checks and a tokamak-to-W7X built-in spread.

- Iterations: map2disc and zeno match the default's count (e.g. 2292, 3281); on the built-ins zeno is within a few iterations, map2disc within ~10%.
- Init cost vs ns: map2disc's guess is ~0.1 to 2.5s and flat in ns; zeno's is 95 to 100% of runtime and grows fast with ns (90s at cma ns=51, 516s at W7X ns=9, vs 1 to 2s solves).
- Shaping (aspect 5.9 to 10.3, elongation 3 to 9, triangularity -0.6 to +0.2): same count as the default, except ~8 to 10% fewer for map2disc on the low-aspect built-ins and ~35% more on W7X.
- JACOBIAN_75_TIMES_BAD: 2 of 50 fail under the default (one is that error); map2disc fails the same way on both.

--- comment by jurasic-pf, 2026-06-01 23:07 UTC ---
>   Shaping (aspect 5.9 to 10.3, elongation 3 to 9, triangularity -0.6 to +0.2): same count as the default, except ~8 to 10% fewer for map2disc on the low-aspect built-ins and ~35% more on W7X.

If this implementation of zenos method is on the same order as the solve, then it doesn't seem practical for our usecase (still very useful information!). map2disc  could be a good candidate for a better initialization.

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-06-01 23:38 UTC ---
Correction: in that sweep the restart guess wasn't reaching the solver, so the map2disc numbers were just the default run. Injected correctly, map2disc is slightly slower on an easy case (cth_like ns=25: 448 vs 439) and still hits `JACOBIAN_75` despite a nested interior (min scaled Jacobian 0.27). Happy to keep exploring in whatever direction you think is most useful.

--- description ---
## Summary

Adds `vmecpp.objectives`, a library of stellarator-optimization figures of merit
evaluated on a converged VMEC++ equilibrium: aspect ratio, the rotational-transform
profile, the magnetic well, the mirror ratio, and the Boozer quasi-symmetry
residual, together with the Boozer-coordinate transform the residual is built on.
Each objective reads a converged `wout` and returns a scalar (or a profile); none
of them touch the solver, so they compose with any equilibrium VMEC++ produces.

The intent is to compute and test optimization targets in Python while the C++
core stays the forward model. Where an objective has a SIMSOPT counterpart the two
agree to numerical precision, so these are usable without a SIMSOPT/VMEC2000 build.

## Objectives

`vmecpp.objectives` exposes:

| Function | Definition |
|---|---|
| `aspect_ratio(eq)` | R0 / a, as reported by VMEC (`wout.aspect`) |
| `iota_profile(eq)` | rotational transform on the full-grid surfaces, returned as `(s, iota)` |
| `iota_profile_residual(eq, target)` | mean-square deviation of iota from a target profile (callable or array) |
| `magnetic_well(eq)` | vacuum well `(V'(s->0) - V'(s->1)) / V'(s->0)`, with `V'` from `dV/dPsi` |
| `mirror_ratio(eq)` | `(Bmax - Bmin) / (Bmax + Bmin)` over a flux surface |
| `field_strength_on_surface(eq)` | `\|B\|(theta, zeta)` on a flux surface, from `bmnc` |
| `boozer_spectrum(eq)` | `\|B\|` Boozer harmonics on a surface |
| `boozer_roundtrip_residual(eq)` | self-consistency residual of the Boozer transform |
| `quasisymmetry_residual(eq, helicity_m, helicity_n)` | symmetry-breaking Boozer-harmonic fraction |

Each accepts a `VmecOutput` or its `VmecWOut`.

## Boozer transform

The quasi-symmetry residual is defined on `|B|` expressed in Boozer angles, so the
PR carries a Boozer-coordinate transform built from the `wout` Fourier data
(Hirshman-Whitson straight-field-line construction):

- `I = Bsub_theta_00` and `G = Bsub_zeta_00` are the enclosed currents;
- the periodic part of the stream function has sine harmonics `w_mn = bsubumnc_mn / m = -bsubvmnc_mn / n`;
- `nu = (w - I*lambda) / (G + iota*I)` gives the Boozer angles `theta_B = theta + lambda + iota*nu`, `zeta_B = zeta + nu`;
- `|B|` is resampled onto Boozer harmonics with the Jacobian weight `J = (1 + lambda_theta)(1 + nu_zeta) + (iota - lambda_zeta)*nu_theta`.

The forward integral runs over a single field period `[0, 2*pi/nfp)`, following the
booz_xform convention. Over one period the toroidal frequency is the mode number
`n` rather than `n*nfp`, so `n_zeta ~ 4*n_boozer` resolves the spectrum with no
toroidal aliasing. The Boozer poloidal content spreads well past the VMEC band, so
`m_boozer` defaults to several times `mpol`.

## Validation

`tests/test_objectives.py` runs every objective on a converged `cth_like_fixed_bdy`
equilibrium and compares against SIMSOPT or against the transform's own
self-consistency:

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| `aspect_ratio` vs `Vmec.aspect()` | exact |
| iota axis / edge / mean vs `Vmec.iota_axis / iota_edge / mean_iota` | < 1e-10 |
| `magnetic_well` vs `Vmec.vacuum_well()` | < 1e-10 |
| `field_strength_on_surface` vs `vmec_compute_geometry().modB` | exact |
| `mirror_ratio` vs the same `\|B\|` field | < 1e-8 |
| Boozer round-trip at `(m, n)_boozer = (48, 20)` | 2.6e-10 |
| Boozer round-trip, `(12, 8)` to `(36, 16)` | 1.3e-4 to 1.1e-8 |
| `quasisymmetry_residual`, stability across truncations | 0.0405 (constant) |

Aspect ratio, iota, the magnetic well, and the mirror-ratio `|B|` field are pinned
to SIMSOPT. The Boozer transform is validated intrinsically: it reconstructs `|B|`
at the VMEC grid points from its own Boozer spectrum, and the residual falls to
machine precision as the mode count grows. The quasi-symmetry residual is constant
under refinement of the Boozer truncation and is cross-checked against simsopt's
real-space `QuasisymmetryRatioResidual`: cth_like is not quasi-axisymmetric, and
both report a finite QA-breaking residual. booz_xform is the conventional external
reference for Boozer spectra; a direct spectral diff against it is a sensible
additional check on top of the self-consistency and SIMSOPT cross-checks here.

## Example

`examples/python_objectives.py` solves the fixed-boundary stellarator and prints
every objective:

```
aspect ratio            : 5.4858
iota on axis / edge     : 1.2522 / 0.8253
magnetic well           : -1.4750e-02
mirror ratio            : 0.4365
quasi-axisymmetry resid : 4.0503e-02
Boozer self-consistency : 3.12e-07
```

--- review by jurasic-pf, 2026-05-31 14:00 UTC: CHANGES_REQUESTED ---
Thanks for the contribution, but implementing optimization specific output quantities is out of scope for `vmecpp`.

`vmecpp` is really intended as a low fidelity, minimum dependency ideal mhd equilibrium solver, which can be wrapped and used by downstream libraries like `simsopt` for optimization, but is not intended to replace any functionality implemented there (aside from the minimal subset in the compatibility module).

There's a medium term effort to decouple `simsopt` and `vmecpp` with the `simsopt` maintainers.

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-05-31 14:06 UTC ---
Makes sense, thanks for the quick read. That fits the simsopt decoupling direction, so I'll close this. Appreciate it.

################################################################################
author: krystophny | opened: 2026-06-14 14:34 UTC | closed: 2026-07-09 20:36 UTC

> **Merge order:** independently mergeable; #582 depends on this PR. Prerequisites through #575 are on `main`.

---

## What

Add the hybrid lambda force (`lambda_force_kernel.h`, #20) to the composed local
map `g` in `local_force_hessian_test.cc` and differentiate the combined
MHD-plus-lambda force density by forward and reverse Enzyme modes. The force
output grows from 12 to 16 blocks (the 12 MHD densities plus `blmn_e/o`,
`clmn_e/o`).

This proves the exact Jacobian `J_g` for the second nonlinear force-density term
of the augmented functional, not just the MHD force chain.

The third term, the spectral-condensation constraint force (#21), carries a
linear Fourier bandpass that couples modes; it is validated end-to-end against
the finite-difference HVP in the pybind exact-HVP path rather than in this
flat-buffer microtest.

## Verification

Built with clang-21 + Enzyme:

```
exact Hessian of VMEC local force map (MHD + lambda kernels)
  geom dofs=3456  force outputs=3072
  reverse dL.v vs finite-diff : 2.55e-08
  forward dL.v vs finite-diff : 2.55e-08
  forward / reverse agreement : 4.06e-15
```

Forward and reverse agree to machine precision and both match central finite
differences to the FD step floor.

Stacked on #21.

## What

Drive a VMEC++ equilibrium from an external optimizer by treating the raw
internal-basis force `F(x)` (PR #7) as the residual `F(x)=0`, and wire it to
general-purpose solvers in `examples/external_optimizers.py`:

- **preconditioned descent** (heavy-ball on VMEC's own preconditioned search
  direction),
- **Jacobian-free Newton-Krylov** (scipy `newton_krylov`).

Force evaluations are counted inside VMEC++ (`force_eval_count`) for a fair
comparison across methods, including evaluations hidden in Krylov matvecs.

## Verification (force evals counted in VMEC++, solovev ns=11)

```
optimizer                  F-evals  iters  time[s]    ||F||      dW
preconditioned descent        2606   1302    0.16   9.9e-10  5.5e-14
Newton-Krylov (JFNK)          2243      0    0.37   2.0e-09  3.3e-13
```

Both converge to the native equilibrium energy. Unpreconditioned JFNK needs more
force evaluations than VMEC's preconditioned descent on this stiff system;
exposing VMEC's preconditioner as an operator so the Krylov solver can use it
(PR #9) cuts JFNK to 507 evals.

Stacked on #7 (internal gradient).

PR #579 [CLOSED, not merged] pybind: expose VMEC preconditioner operator + preconditioned JFNK
################################################################################
author: krystophny | opened: 2026-06-14 14:35 UTC | closed: 2026-07-09 12:56 UTC
branch: itpplasma:expose-preconditioner -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/579
files (4): examples/external_optimizers.py (added) +195 -0, vmecpp/vmec/pybind11/pybind_vmec.cc +24 -0, tests/test_external_optimizers.py (added) +80 -0, tests/test_preconditioner.py (added) +63 -0

--- description ---
> **Stacked PR — part 16/19 of the differentiable-VMEC++ series.** merge after #578 (external-optimizers).
> Diff is cumulative (includes ancestor commits) because the branches are stacked on the fork; review the net change described below.

---

## What

Expose VMEC's hand-built preconditioner as a reusable linear operator and use it
to precondition the external Newton-Krylov solver.

- `VmecModel.apply_preconditioner(v)`: applies VMEC's block preconditioner
  `M^-1` (the m=1, radial, and lambda blocks) -- its approximate inverse Hessian.
  Exact application: `M^-1 . F_raw == F_preconditioned`. It requires a prior
  `evaluate(precondition=true)` (which assembles the radial block); the operator
  is then state-invariant and can be frozen across a Krylov solve.
- `solve_newton_krylov(..., preconditioned=True)`: uses `M^-1` as the inner
  Krylov preconditioner.

This preconditioner is the metric for the preconditioned Krylov / quasi-Newton
solvers and the inner preconditioner for the Hessian solve in the adjoint
sensitivities (#11), and it is why first-order JFNK is so eval-efficient.

## Verification (force evals counted in VMEC++, ns=11)

```
optimizer                  F-evals  time[s]    ||F||      dW
Newton-Krylov (JFNK)          2243     0.37   2.0e-09  3.3e-13
JFNK + M^-1 (this PR)          507     0.08   2.5e-10  2.7e-15   (solovev)
JFNK + M^-1 (this PR)         1633     1.99   2.9e-09  2.1e-09   (cth_like)
```

Preconditioning cuts JFNK from 2243 to **507 force evaluations on solovev (4.4x)**
and converges cth_like where unpreconditioned JFNK does not. This is the
best-of-breed solver in the stack; the exact autodiff HVP (#23) is the operator
that needs no force evaluation per matvec.

Stacked on #8 (external optimizers).

Tracking: #590

--- review by jurasic-pf, 2026-06-24 07:12 UTC: APPROVED ---

PR #583 [CLOSED, not merged] ci: get the test + benchmark workflows green (VMEC2000-from-source, fork-PR benchmark guard)
################################################################################
author: krystophny | opened: 2026-06-14 15:24 UTC | closed: 2026-06-15 06:13 UTC
branch: itpplasma:ci-bazel-build-fixes -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/583
files (3): .github/workflows/benchmarks.yaml +5 -1, .github/workflows/tests.yaml +23 -5, tests/test_simsopt_compat.py +5 -0

--- description ---
Scope: CI only, standalone. No source or build-system changes. Independent of
the differentiable-VMEC++ PR stack (#564-#582); please review and merge this one
first so those PRs inherit green CI from `main`.

## What it fixes

**1. VMEC2000 compatibility test (`test_ensure_vmec2000_input_from_vmecpp_input`).**
The pinned `vmec-0.0.6` cp310 wheel was f90wrapped against numpy 1.x. The test
env now resolves numpy 2.x, under which importing that wheel dies in the f90wrap
array interface:

```
ValueError: _vmec._vmec.f90wrap_vmec_input__array__rbc:
  failed to create array ... 0-th dimension must be fixed to 2 but got 4
```

So the test could never actually run. (It is red on `main` too: the wheel's
runtime libs - libnetcdff, scalapack, openblas - are not installed there, and
pytest 9's `importorskip` only skips `ModuleNotFoundError`, so a missing `.so`
is a hard failure rather than a skip.)

Fix: build VMEC2000 from upstream source with current `f90wrap`, which produces
numpy-2-compatible bindings. The recipe mirrors SIMSOPT's own CI
(`hiddenSymmetries/VMEC2000`, `cmake/machines/ubuntu.json`). No fork needed. An
explicit `import vmec` check in the install step surfaces any remaining problem
there rather than as a confusing test failure.

**2. Benchmark workflow on fork PRs.** The result-upload step needs a writable
`GITHUB_TOKEN`; on fork PRs the token is read-only, so it failed with
"Resource not accessible by integration". Gate that step to same-repo PRs; the
benchmark itself still runs.

## Verification

Failing before (ubuntu-22.04, py3.10): `import vmec` chain broke on
libnetcdff -> libscalapack -> libopenblas, then on the f90wrap/numpy-2
`rbc` ValueError above.

Passing after: VMEC2000 builds from source, `import vmec` -> `VMEC2000 import OK`,
and the compatibility test runs. Benchmark workflow no longer errors on the
upload step for fork PRs.

PR #585 [CLOSED, not merged] geometry: expose direct VMEC++ evaluation through ISO C
################################################################################
author: krystophny | opened: 2026-06-15 06:30 UTC | closed: 2026-08-30 19:29 UTC

--- description ---
Stack order: 3/6, targets `main`; merge after #584.

Adds the stable C-facing layer used by Fortran and other non-Python clients.

- creates an opaque geometry handle directly from a VMEC input file and runs VMEC++ in memory
- returns R, Z, lambda, toroidal flux, poloidal flux, and the common ten-entry spatial jet
- contains exceptions at the ABI boundary and reports thread-local errors
- installs the C header and exports the symbols from `vmecpp_core`

The end-to-end test runs VMEC++ and checks the LCFS against the input boundary Fourier series, independently of wout.

--- review by jurasic-pf, 2026-08-23 18:25 UTC: CHANGES_REQUESTED ---
Hi Chris, this is a very impactful demonstration, and the community probably wants to jump on this asap. I certainly know I do.

All the more I think getting the API right the first time around is important. This approach for implementing adjoints requires a significant amount of custom code for different objectives. This particular PR works for QS, but not for different implementations of QI, turbulence proxies, or anything else.
In that aspect it's similar to the implementation approach taken in E.J.Pauls adjoint VMEC paper https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.10028 https://github.com/ejpaul/ALPOpt , but maybe we can learn from that and improve.

Can we design the interface such that researchers can build whatever objectives they want on top of `vmecpp` and get adjoint gradients for free? The clearest path I see towards that is to wrap the a solve like function (probably not feasible to do for the full `vmecpp.run`) in a `custom_vjp` https://docs.jax.dev/en/latest/jep/2026-custom-derivatives.html . A QS objective built in JAX, and only taking geometry state variables  would then naturally propagate the vjp through to the `custom_vjp` boundary, from where enzyme takes over.

--- comment by krystophny, 2026-08-30 19:29 UTC ---
Agreed - closing this. You maintain the C++ API, we maintain the adapter.

It is already done and working. The adapter now lives in SIMPLE (`itpplasma/SIMPLE#521`), which builds VMEC++ from source via `FetchContent` pinned to #584's commit `62be1ee`. Verified from deleted build directories: clean configure+build, both `test_vmecpp_field_parity` and `test_vmecpp_orbit_parity` pass, and the default build fetches and compiles no VMEC++ at all. The Fortran side needed no change - the C ABI is ours now, so it is ours to keep stable.

Note we are **not** asking you to install headers or export a CMake package. Building from source sidesteps that entirely, so nothing on your side has to grow to support us.

The one-line `.coefficients = {}` designated-initializer completion that lived here is folded down into #584.

Two things I hit doing this, both small and both useful to you independently of us:

1. **`vmecpp_core` has no `target_include_directories(... PUBLIC ...)`.** Include paths come from directory-scoped `include_directories()` calls, so they do not propagate to any target outside the vmecpp subtree. The link interface is already correct (`absl::*`, HDF5, netCDF, LAPACK, OpenMP, nlohmann_json are all PUBLIC) - it is only the include side that has to be hand-wired by a consumer.

2. **`EIGEN_MAX_ALIGN_BYTES=32` / `EIGEN_MAX_STATIC_ALIGN_BYTES=32` are set with directory-scoped `add_compile_definitions()`.** This one is a trap rather than an inconvenience: a consumer target that misses them compiles cleanly and then aborts at runtime with `double free or corruption (out)` in `Eigen::internal::aligned_free` when a `vmecpp::VmecINDATA` is destroyed. Your own comment in `CMakeLists.txt` already says this pin is ABI-affecting, so anything that can reach a vmecpp type across a TU boundary needs it.

Both would be fixed by putting the include dirs and those two definitions on the `vmecpp_core` target interface. Happy to send that as a small standalone PR if you want it - say the word and I will.

Separately, `_vmecpp` is built unconditionally with no option to skip it, so a C++-only consumer pays a pybind11 fetch and a Python-dev dependency for nothing. A `VMECPP_BUILD_PYTHON` option would be welcome, but that one is genuinely just convenience.

The design objection in your 23 Aug review is answered in #707 (QS as 126 lines of pure Python, no C++) and #710 (the generic solve VJP). The stack is now #584 -> #703 -> #707 -> #710.

PR #601 [CLOSED, not merged] Sparse Fourier coefficients via extra geometry modes and sparse_lambda
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-06-27 01:49 UTC | closed: 2026-07-28 23:42 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:issue-315-sparse-fourier -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/601
files (10): src/vmecpp/__init__.py +32 -0, vmecpp/common/sizes/sizes.cc +18 -1, vmecpp/common/sizes/sizes.h +58 -0, vmecpp/common/vmec_indata/vmec_indata.cc +55 -0, vmecpp/common/vmec_indata/vmec_indata.h +16 -0, vmecpp/vmec/fourier_coefficients/fourier_coefficients.cc +19 -2, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/dft_toroidal.cc +43 -20, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +9 -0, vmecpp/vmec/pybind11/pybind_vmec.cc +6 -1, tests/test_extra_geometry_modes.py (added) +181 -0

--- description ---
Closes #315.

## Motivation

#315 asks to "only consider non-zero Fourier coefficients in the computation," to cheaply explore high-frequency perturbations to an equilibrium and global perturbations whose toroidal mode numbers are not multiples of NFP.

Today the mode set is the dense grid: poloidal `m` in `[0, mpol)`, toroidal `n` in `[-ntor, ntor]`, with the toroidal basis hard-wired to `n*nfp`. Adding a single high-`m` mode, or a non-axisymmetric mode at `nfp=1`, forces the full dense grid and pays for every intermediate mode even though it stays at (or near) zero.

This adds an opt-in sparse mode set: the geometry (and, with `sparse_lambda`, lambda) is restricted to an explicit set of free modes, and the Fourier transforms iterate only those modes. A reduced-resolution base equilibrium can then carry a few explicit high-frequency or global modes, computed at a fraction of the dense cost, while everything else is frozen at zero.

## Approach

Two inputs build on the geometry masking from #598 (`mpol_geometry`/`ntor_geometry` freeze R/Z above a cap):

- `extra_geometry_m` / `extra_geometry_n`: an explicit list of `(m, n)` geometry modes kept free *above* the cap. The active geometry set is the cap rectangle plus these extras; everything else is frozen at zero each iteration (force and state), so a single perturbation mode can be added to a converged base and studied in isolation.
- `sparse_lambda`: also restricts lambda to the active set, which lets the transforms skip the frozen modes. The frozen modes are exactly zero, so skipping their work is identical to the dense transform:
  - whole poloidal rows with no free mode are skipped in the inverse and forward DFTs (2D and 3D);
  - the toroidal sums run only up to the highest free `n` at each `m`.

  The preconditioner still masks the frozen modes to zero (correct, computed-then-discarded); skipping its per-mode solve as well is left out because a frozen mode's force is already zero, so it contributes nothing.

Global, non-NFP perturbations are reached by running `nfp=1` (the full torus, where every integer `n` is a valid toroidal mode) with a sparse mode set, so the cost of the otherwise-dense high-`ntor` grid is avoided.

When neither input is set (the default), nothing is masked and the transforms run the dense path unchanged.

## Changes

- `vmec_indata`: `extra_geometry_m` / `extra_geometry_n` and `sparse_lambda` inputs, with JSON and HDF5 read/write and an `extra_geometry_*` length-consistency check.
- `sizes`: derive the active-mode mask and `isActiveGeometryMode` / `anyActiveGeometryAtM` / `maxActiveGeometryNAtM` helpers.
- `fourier_coefficients`: `maskGeometryAbove` keeps the extra modes and, under `sparse_lambda`, also freezes lambda.
- `dft_toroidal` / `ideal_mhd_model`: the inverse and forward symmetric DFTs (2D and 3D) skip frozen poloidal rows and shorten the toroidal sums under `sparse_lambda`.
- `pybind_vmec.cc` / `__init__.py`: expose the three inputs.

## Validation

Skipping the frozen modes is bit-identical to the dense (masked) transform: on solovev with a geometry cap and a kept `m=4` extra, the converged MHD energy is `2.546537` with and without the poloidal-row skip; on an `nfp=1` run with a global `(m=1, n=1)` mode it is `2.549049` with and without the toroidal-sum shortening.

Cost, single grid `ns=51`, `mpol=60`, 600 iterations: the dense run takes `0.75 s`; the same base with the geometry capped at `m<3` and `sparse_lambda` takes `0.49 s` (the transform no longer touches the 57 frozen rows; the remaining cost is the real-space physics on the grid, which a high mode still requires).

The non-NFP capability runs end to end: a global `n=1` mode on an axisymmetric base at `nfp=1`, kept as a sparse extra, converges to `ftol = 1e-11` in 197 iterations.

Default (no extras, `sparse_lambda=False`) is a no-op: the #598 `test_lambda_resolution.py` cases, including the cap-at-full-resolution array-for-array equality, are unchanged.

## Test

`tests/test_extra_geometry_modes.py`:
- `test_extra_mode_kept_above_geometry_cap` / `test_only_listed_extra_modes_are_kept`: a high-`m` perturbation is frozen to zero under the cap, kept (matching the full-resolution run) when listed as an extra mode, and only the listed modes are kept;
- `test_sparse_lambda_freezes_lambda_above_cap`: `sparse_lambda` freezes lambda above the cap to exactly zero, where it otherwise carries content;
- `test_non_nfp_global_perturbation_runs_sparsely`: an `nfp=1` global `(m=1, n=1)` mode is kept while the frozen `n=2` modes stay at zero.

PR #616 [CLOSED, not merged] 2D block-tridiagonal preconditioner
################################################################################
author: CharlesCNorton | opened: 2026-07-08 20:52 UTC | closed: 2026-07-28 23:38 UTC
branch: CharlesCNorton:prec2d -> main
url: https://github.com/proximafusion/vmecpp/pull/616
files (9): vmecpp/common/CMakeLists.txt +1 -0, vmecpp/common/block_tridiagonal/BUILD.bazel (added) +24 -0, vmecpp/common/block_tridiagonal/CMakeLists.txt (added) +5 -0, vmecpp/common/block_tridiagonal/block_tridiagonal.cc (added) +106 -0, vmecpp/common/block_tridiagonal/block_tridiagonal.h (added) +87 -0, vmecpp/common/block_tridiagonal/block_tridiagonal_test.cc (added) +128 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/BUILD.bazel +1 -0, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.cc +90 -12, vmecpp/vmec/ideal_mhd_model/ideal_mhd_model.h +5 -0

--- description ---
VMEC++'s convergence preconditioner is the per-mode radial operator assembled in `computePreconditioningMatrix` and applied in `applyRZPreconditioner`: for each Fourier mode `(m, n)` it solves a scalar tridiagonal system down the radial grid, the poloidal and toroidal mode numbers entering only as diagonal `m²` and `(n·nfp)²` weights. Different modes are never coupled to one another. That approximation is cheap and robust, but it is also the one that weakens on strongly-shaped, high-mode-number equilibria, where the true Hessian couples the poloidal modes within a flux surface. PARVMEC handles this with `precon2d`/BCYCLIC: a dense per-surface block coupling all poloidal modes, with the surfaces tied together radially into a block-tridiagonal system. This PR lands the block-tridiagonal solver and the wiring that reshapes the R/Z preconditioner into that form; the mode-coupling coefficients that make it a genuine 2D preconditioner follow on this branch.

## What

**`common/block_tridiagonal`** — a standalone, dependency-light package:

- `BlockTridiagonalFactorization` — block-Thomas factorization and solve for `L_j x_{j-1} + D_j x_j + U_j x_{j+1} = b_j` over `n` block rows of size `k`. The forward sweep stores the explicit inverse of each reduced pivot block, so the many right-hand sides that share one operator (one per Fourier basis function) reuse a single factorization.
- `AssembleFdBlockTridiagonal` — assembles the Jacobian `dF/dx` of a force field with nearest-neighbour radial coupling by central finite differences. Perturbing surface `j` moves the force only on surfaces `j-1, j, j+1`, so surfaces three apart are perturbed together and the assembly costs `6·k` force evaluations independent of the number of surfaces.
- Unit tests for both, checked against a known analytic solution, a dense `(n·k)×(n·k)` reference solve, and an analytic Jacobian, across a range of block counts and block sizes.

**`applyRZPreconditioner`** — an opt-in path, `solveBlockRZPreconditioner`, that regroups the R/Z preconditioner as one block-tridiagonal system per toroidal mode number `n`: the poloidal modes of a surface form each block, the radial grid indexes the block rows, and the system is solved with the new factorization. It is gated behind `VMECPP_PREC2D`; the per-mode scalar solve remains the default and is untouched.

## Verification

- With only the block diagonals populated from the existing 1D coefficients, the block operator is algebraically the per-mode scalar solve, so the two converge to the same equilibrium up to floating-point round-off. On `cth_like_fixed_bdy` and `solovev`, converged `volume_p` and `aspect` match the default to all printed digits and `betatotal` and the magnetic-axis position agree to `~1e-12`, with `VMECPP_PREC2D` set and unset; the residual difference is the block-Thomas sweep's arithmetic ordering.
- `BlockTridiagonalFactorization` matches both the analytic solution and the dense reference solve to machine precision (`~1e-14`); `AssembleFdBlockTridiagonal` recovers the analytic Jacobian of a known nearest-neighbour force field.
- Full CMake build; Bazel targets and deps added for the new package. clang-format and clang-tidy clean.

## Follow-up

The off-diagonal poloidal coupling is the angle-resolved form of the same metric integrals that `computePreconditioningMatrix` currently angle-averages into the per-mode diagonal (`ax`/`bx` summed over the poloidal grid). Adding those harmonics to the block off-diagonals, together with the second-order solve that consumes them, turns this into the full 2D preconditioner and lands next on this branch.

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-07-09 01:41 UTC ---
@jurasic-pf

Status on the off-diagonal coupling.

The block-tridiagonal solver and FD assembler reproduce the per-mode 1D preconditioner exactly (the committed state). Coupling attempts so far:

- Analytic off-diagonal from the angle-resolved `ax` (radial) metric integrand: converges to the same equilibrium but takes more force iterations than the 1D (cth_like 147 vs 121; solovev 375 vs 204). A runtime scan over coupling scale and sign found no value that lowered the count.
- Anderson acceleration of the 1D-preconditioned fixed point `x ← x + M⁻¹F`: converges but does not fall below the native momentum iteration on the cases tested (cth_like 285 vs 239; cma ns=15 ≈1700 vs 635).
- A full 1D-preconditioned Hessian inverse (FD Jacobian, SVD-truncated) lowers the iteration count substantially (cma ≈40 vs ≈500) at O(N³) solve and O(N) evaluations per update; its wall-clock at production resolution is not yet measured.

Remaining steps:

**Complete the explicit coupling**
- Add the `bx` (m²) poloidal-curvature term to the block off-diagonal (current coupling is `ax` radial only).
- Add the R/Z cross-coupling and the odd/mixed-parity cross terms (current coupling is same-parity, per-component).
- Validate the assembled coupling against the true finite-difference Hessian off-diagonal.
- Fix the multi-threaded `axmat` sizing (full-range storage + per-partition fill) so it runs threaded.
- Re-scan coupling scale and sign once the coupling is complete.

**History-based / quasi-Newton acceleration**
- Anderson combined with the native momentum rather than replacing it.
- Tuned Anderson: relaxation factor, least-squares regularization, restart depth.
- L-BFGS two-loop recursion on the 1D-preconditioned residual.
- Limited-memory Broyden.

**SVD-truncated Hessian**
- Measure its wall-clock at production resolution against the native iteration.
- Replace the 2N-evaluation dense FD Jacobian with the stride-3 block-tridiagonal FD (≈6k evaluations).
- Replace the full SVD with a rank-revealing or iterative solve.
- Characterize the rcond-threshold sensitivity and the near-null m=0 radial subspace.

**Cheaper inner solves (contingent on a helpful coupling)**
- Banded block solve exploiting low |m−m′| coupling.
- Woodbury low-rank correction to the 1D block.

**Alternative constructions**
- Alternating-direction split: 1D radial solve, then a per-surface poloidal solve.
- Mode-space multigrid.

**Coverage and infrastructure**
- Test strongly-shaped and high-mode-number cases (W7-X, the #157 input) and larger ns/mpol.
- Add a wall-clock benchmark harness (iterations and time).

--- comment by CharlesCNorton, 2026-07-09 20:22 UTC ---
@jurasic-pf

- A full 1D-preconditioned Hessian inverse (FD Jacobian, SVD-truncated), evaluated on #626's history-independent raw force, carries real coarse-tolerance curvature (cth_like reaches 1e-4 in 8 steps, versus 67 for the native) but stalls before tight convergence, fails to reach even 1e-4 on cma, and does not converge when used as a momentum-iteration preconditioner. The near-null m=0 subspace defeats the truncated inverse near convergence, and the dense assembly costs roughly 10x the native solve, so it is not a usable accelerator.
